<a id="page-430"></a>

## עמוד 430

Only part of these types carry on to the ensuing periods. The most outstanding Middle Bronze water systems were natural springs surrounded by city walls. Concomitantly, there was continuity and evolution of cisterns, quarried projects and aqueducts. In the Late Bronze Age and Iron Age 1 there was a decline in quantity, quality and variety of water systems, except those found in Mycene, which are not tied to the water culture of Israel. The Iron Age 2 defined a climax in the evolution of water systems, with abundant sites and significant variety. Most outstanding are siege water projects dug to great depths and many cisterns. The quarried water projects are on a scale impressive by any standards and possibly were a model for imitation in other countries.

Detailed plaster inspections show that starting in the Early Bronze Age, clayey soil was utilized as hydraulic plaster. In the Middle Bronze-Age, fractures in cisterns were being waterproofed, and in the Late Bronze Age plaster fully coated cisterns and reservoirs walls. Until the Iron Age 2, plaster was white, based on lime alone, while in Iron Age 2 period, ash and clays were being added to the plaster, giving it gray, pale brown or yellow hues.

After the climax of water projects in the Iron Age 2, there came a decline in the Persian period, and emergence of different water projects starting in the Hellenistic period. These were the long distance aqueducts, which were introduced to Israel together with Hellenistic culture, following the Macedonian conquest. Siege water projects made way for aqueducts, and the water culture in Israel changed its character.

The present investigation bears on the central themes of water culture in Israel during the ancient period. Most of the data on water projects was hard to come by for several reasons. To begin with, many investigators do not pay enough attention during excavations to inspection and description of cisterns. Proper inspection occurs only when an impressive water project is being discovered. Second reason is that water projects are usually found in low lying areas and are rarely seen before commencement of excavations. Suffice to mention that the impressive water projects of Tel Be'er Sheba and Tel Bet Shemesh were discovered only in recent years. It is hoped that future excavations will unearth more unique water projects, which will shed additional light on this fascinating topic.

---

<a id="page-431"></a>

## עמוד 431

The first two types are manifested in 17 sites with 20 water systems. These are the main monumental water projects found in Israel during the Biblical period. In most of them, thousands of cubic meters of rock had to be quarried. Their magnitude and centrality suggest they were executed by the central regimes of the kingdoms of Judea and Israel. Water wells were found in only two sites, and it seems that they were overtaken by water projects for times of siege and cisterns, while other wells, dug outside settlements, are hard to find or date accurately.

Cisterns were found in 20 sites or regions. In most of them, the number of cisterns is significant and attests to an increasingly prevalent method during Iron Age 2, which had the advantage of bringing drainage water to the door step. The large regal water projects for times of siege were performed as part of the services rendered by the government to its citizens, while digging of cisterns was private enterprise by the inhabitants of the houses. The average volume of cisterns in Iron Age 2 was 20-30 cubic meters, which sufficed to a family of five people, consuming about 5 cubic meters per person per year. It looks like the central monumental water projects were built first, only later followed by family cisterns.

Long distance aqueducts made their first appearance during Iron Age 2. These aqueducts were present in adjacent countries, and their presence attests to a breakthrough which made its debut later in Israel.

The abundance and variety of water systems in Israel and its neighbors in Iron Age 2 indicate imaginative cultural evolution which led to result that the optimal water supply solution was tailored to the needs of each settlement. This approach was unique. In the periods that followed, long distance water projects dominated the scene.

Classification of water systems based on the type in a periodical cross section shows that the most abundant water project throughout the ages was the well. Affinity to springs is present mainly in the earlier periods. Starting with the Early Bronze Age additional new types appeared, like a pond or a drainage water reservoir within the city walls, dams for water storage, cisterns and perhaps aqueduct and quarried water project.

---

<a id="page-432"></a>

## עמוד 432

impermeable plaster covered cisterns walls completely, and this seems to be the main contribution of the Late Bronze Age to water works evolution.

In Iron Age 1, there were almost no new water systems. Four wells, few cisterns, water system for siege period which uses a hidden route to a spring and a possible aqueduct are known from this period. Lack of big cities from this period, excluding those cities of the Sea Peoples, is the reason for scarcity of water systems. The main works are present in sites with presence of the Philistines. This could be another sign for the high technological level of these people, evident from other disciplines. The well in Tel Be'er Sheba, 70 meters deep, is the deepest known ancient well in Israel. It is hard to accept such an early age for such a deep well, but this is the best possible interpretation of the dating.

Dearth of cisterns in sites of the Iron Age 1 led to other solutions to water plight of settlers in hot regions. It could be that Zertal proposal, according to which the inhabitants used 'collared rim' jars to store water was the solution to this problem. In the 11th century B.C.E. the number of these jars diminished, while the number of cisterns increased. Dependency on cisterns increased towards the 10th century B.C.E. when their usage became quite common.

The Iron Age 2 was a climax period in this aspect and it boasted large population, many large well planned cities, advanced technology, monumental architecture, and impressive water systems. In this period, all types of water systems from the previous periods were manifested. There were five types of water systems:
1) Urban water systems from springs and groundwater for times of peace and war.
2) Water reservoirs fed by external aqueduct in cities and fortresses for times of war and peace.
3) Wells
4) Cisterns
5) Aqueducts

---

<a id="page-433"></a>

## עמוד 433

water wells were used as well, and it is only due to coincidence or due to inability to clearly identify them, that they have not been encountered. Some cities were built in proximity to springs. Thus 'Afeq was built on the springs of the Yarkon River, surrounded on two sides at least by a pond formed by fresh water, while Jerusalem was built near the Gihon spring even before it was fortified.

In the Middle Bronze Age, there were diverse types of water systems. The first is a spring that was incorporated into the city walls. The origin of this method is in Syria, and it was introduced into fortified cities throughout Israel. In all these cities, spring outflow was larger than consumption, and means of getting rid of the surplus outside city walls had to be devised. In Jerusalem, fortifications were built above the spring, which helped to incorporate it within the city walls. Other water systems were hewn in the rock down to ground water table. These were first of their kind and indicate rock quarrying on a scale typical of state organization. Water wells were also common. The deepest known well, 44 meters deep, was found in Lachish.

Water cisterns found in Hazor and Gezer attest to drainage water collection. For the first time there was plaster, used only to water proof cracks, but it is an evidence for initial usage of plaster in cisterns. The variety of water works attest to the technological, organizational and cultural might of this period. To sum up: Cities with walls around springs, pits dug to groundwater table, wells, cisterns and possibly the first aqueduct (in Mari) were typical of this period.

In the Late Bronze Age, evolution of water systems essentially came to a halt. This is evident from the small number of sites with water systems, and the limited variety of water systems from this period. While continued usage of water works from the Middle Bronze Age is possible, no corroborating evidence for this has been found. In two adjacent cultures, in Mycene and Egypt, huge water systems were present, which seemed to continue the technology evident in the Middle Bronze Age in Israel, although no cultural connection with these cultures is known. Usage of cisterns continued, and in Hazor and Ta'anach, water reservoirs were built. Distinctly from earlier periods,

---

<a id="page-434"></a>

## עמוד 434

Other settlements from this period are found near water springs which persisted to the present. Location of other settlements like Gilgal, Netiv Hagdud and Beidha in areas that are waterless today, prompted investigators to look for relics and hints of an ancient water source. These settlements indicate a different water regime and higher precipitation during this period than today.

Usage of wells continued in the Pottery Neolithic period, as proven by findings in sites along the Carmel coast. In one of these sites, Kfar Samir, a well was found, built from wood and stones in a manner unknown from earlier or later sites. There is only sparse data on this period and few excavations of similar sites.

From the Chalcolithic period, only two water wells are known, excavated in the valley of Beer Sheva. The great concentration of Chalcolithic sites in the valleys of Beer Sheva and Arad lead to the inference that creeks were more water affluent than they are today, and that this region had ample water wells. In other regions, where no wells were found, there is close proximity of settlements to water springs. There were no fortified settlements in this period, and hence no need for water works for supply during sieges.

In the Early Bronze Age, the first cities were built, and as part of the overall planning, water systems that collected drainage water to the lowest part of the cities were built. These water systems were located within the city walls, and were used during siege periods as well. In the water pond in ‘Ai, hydraulic plaster, made of red, water impermeable clayey soil, was found. One reason for the paucity of data on water systems from this period is that their layers are usually buried under layers of later periods.

Collection of drainage water into cisterns dug in soft chalky limestone was initiated during this period. Ease of quarrying and the water impermeability of this rock helped promote cistern hewing even before hydraulic plaster was discovered. In the fortified cities, central water ponds were built, while in the smaller settlements, small cisterns for familial consumption were common.

It looks as if the climate was more humid, evaporation lower, and water collected in ponds were transferred after rains to large jars, similar to those found in 'Arad. No doubt

---

<a id="page-435"></a>

## עמוד 435

represents optimal consumption and under duress can be cut in half. It should be noted that present day consumption in Israel is 110 cmpy, and it cannot be curtailed because of the rise in living standards.

Definitions of the various water sources, both natural and artificial, were given in the introduction. In these definitions, the difference between underground reservoir and a pool (pond) was clarified. A pool (pond) is an open, wide and shallow water body, while an underground reservoir is a particularly large cistern with a volume of few hundred cubic meters at least.

Past research on water projects was usually carried out when, within the framework of a general dig in a site, a water project was found. In such a case, it would have been published as part of the dig's general report. Most of these publications constitute only small portions of the general reports and only infrequently, as in Megiddo and Gibeon, did a separate publication appear. Even these publications focused in most cases on the specific water project and did not delve into comparisons with other projects. The publications where such comparisons were made, such as those by Vincent, Amiran, Cole and Shiloh refer only to monumental water projects. As for other types of water projects, such as small reservoirs and cisterns, the data usually released on them in dig reports is of the most basic type and does not provide essential information such as volume, types of plaster etc.

- In the present study, I've touched the core questions which define the physical survival of humans and their vital need for water. Without water there is no life, and where water was missing, humans had to purchase it. Water collection in Israel was an inalienable part of the climatic nature of this geographic province - a province with different climate zones, ranging between extremes. Water scarcity and semi-arid climate, as well as presence of deserts, brought humans, from the Pre-pottery Neolithic period onward, to build water systems. The first wells in the ancient world were found in 'Atlit Yam and they are testify to the human knowledge and capacity for procuring water. While the presence of two wells in a village seems obvious in present day terms, the 8000 years age of the village and wells make it quite unique. This is a rare discovery which presents the first steps. Nothing can be said about earlier water systems without proven findings. If there were wells prior to 'Atlit Yam, they were probably dug in river beds. The wells of Atlit Yam, about 6 meters deep, belong to an advanced stage of well digging and quarrying. Their presence attests to the hydrological and engineering knowledge, as well as the building and quarrying abilities of the inhabitants of 'Atlit Yam.

---

<a id="page-436"></a>

## עמוד 436 - Summary

# Summary

This research on ancient water systems in settlements in Israel encompasses the period between the years circa 6000 to 600 B.C.E. Its main purpose was to reconnoiter and survey the various water systems which existed during this extended period, present the field evidence and wrestle with the resulting interpretation for each water system. A second purpose was to assemble all the data and reach general conclusions about the character of water works in each specific period and the evolution of water systems throughout the various periods and discuss general focal themes.

Few background themes are surveyed in the introduction chapter. These themes will be the basis for discussion of the field evidence in the concluding chapters. The following conclusions were deduced:

-   **1) Climate** Israel is located within a semi-arid region with irregular precipitation. It rains only during six months out of the year, a fact which forced the inhabitants to find a solution to ensure constant water supply by means of different water systems. Various diverse solutions have been invented in Israel to tackle water scarcity. Specific solutions were found in each region.

    Observable climatic variations during the six thousand years covered by this survey were minimal. It is quite obvious that during the Pre-pottery Neolithic B, Chalcolithic and Early Bronze periods a more humid climate prevailed in Israel. The impact of these climatic variations on water availability and the research topics is minimal, and no quantitative estimation of the magnitude of this change is possible.

    I had to limit myself to presentation of the different investigations into climate changes, present the facts and lament the situation whereby no quantitative results can be incorporated into the present research. The main conclusion is that throughout the extended period covered by this work, climate was broadly similar to that of the present day, with the exception of three more humid sub periods, in which water scarcities were alleviated, although it cannot be concluded to what extent.

-   **2) Water consumption** Investigations by various authors, on various époques and places, have led to a host of estimates and data about personal yearly water consumption. Discussion of all these investigations led me to propose that in the extended period covered here, personal water consumption was about 5 cubic meters per year (cmpy). This is somewhat lower than my previous estimate for the Roman and Byzantine periods which was about 7 cmpy. The figure of 5 cmpy does not cover consumption by domesticated animals. When this additional component is factored in, yearly water consumption per person rises to 30 cubic meters. This figure
